## Naomi · 2026-06-22T17:34:58.122Z

walk me through how to get that

## Codex · 2026-06-22T17:35:53.438Z · final_answer

Yes. We need two things:

1. **Developer ID Application certificate** in your Keychain  
2. **Notary credentials** stored in Keychain for `notarytool`

Apple says Developer ID is what Gatekeeper uses for apps distributed outside the Mac App Store, and notarization adds the ticket Gatekeeper trusts. Apple also says Developer ID cert creation requires the Account Holder role. Sources: [Developer ID support](https://developer.apple.com/support/developer-id/), [Developer ID certificates](https://developer.apple.com/help/account/certificates/create-developer-id-certificates/), [Signing apps for Gatekeeper](https://developer.apple.com/developer-id/).

**Step 1: Create/install the Developer ID Application cert**

Easiest way:

1. Open **Xcode**
2. Top menu: **Xcode → Settings…**
3. Go to **Accounts**
4. Select your Apple ID / team
5. Click **Manage Certificates…**
6. Click **+**
7. Choose **Developer ID Application**
8. Let Xcode create it

Then tell me “done” and I’ll verify with:

```bash
security find-identity -v -p codesigning
```

We need to see something like:

```text
Developer ID Application: Naomi Ivie (...)
```

Right now you only have `Apple Development`, which is not enough for public Mac distribution.

**Step 2: Get notarization credentials**

Best route: App Store Connect API key.

1. Go to [App Store Connect](https://appstoreconnect.apple.com/)
2. Open **Users and Access**
3. Go to **Integrations**
4. Select **App Store Connect API**
5. Choose **Team Keys**
6. Click **+** / generate key
7. Name it: `Sage Notarization`
8. Access/role: choose **App Manager** if available
9. Generate it
10. Download the `.p8` file immediately; Apple only lets you download it once
11. Keep these three things:
    - `.p8` file
    - **Key ID**
    - **Issuer ID**

Then I’ll store it safely in your Keychain with:

```bash
xcrun notarytool store-credentials "sage-notary" \
  --key "/path/to/AuthKey_XXXX.p8" \
  --key-id "KEYID" \
  --issuer "ISSUER-ID"
```

After that I can rebuild Sage, sign with Developer ID, submit to Apple notarization, staple the ticket, and produce the real public DMG.

## Codex · 2026-06-22T17:37:58.647Z · commentary

That screenshot is the exact PLA blocker, not a certificate problem yet. Xcode can’t create the Developer ID certificate until the latest Apple Developer Program License Agreement is accepted in your developer account.

## Codex · 2026-06-22T17:38:10.393Z · final_answer

This is fixable. Do this first:

1. Go to [developer.apple.com/account](https://developer.apple.com/account)
2. Sign in with the Apple ID that owns the developer membership.
3. Look for a top banner or alert saying the **Apple Developer Program License Agreement** has been updated.
4. Click **Review Agreement**.
5. Scroll through it and click **I Agree** / **Accept**.
6. Log out and back in if Apple still shows the warning.
7. Quit Xcode completely and reopen it.
8. Go back to **Xcode → Settings → Accounts → Manage Certificates → + → Developer ID Application**.

Important: Apple says Developer ID cert creation requires the **Account Holder** role. If your Apple ID is only an admin/member, the Account Holder has to accept the PLA first.

Once you accept it, tell me “done,” and I’ll check the terminal for:

```bash
security find-identity -v -p codesigning
```

We’re looking for `Developer ID Application: ...`, then I can sign/notarize Sage.

- Finance
- Developer, unless App Manager/Admin are unavailable

- **Key ID**
- **Issuer ID**

## Codex · 2026-06-22T18:13:40.896Z · commentary

Yes. The important distinction is: Apple accepted the file upload, but the notarization decision is still pending until the status becomes `Accepted` or `Invalid`. I can show you the exact Terminal path, and I’ll stop my local waiter so you don’t have two terminals watching the same job.

## Codex · 2026-06-22T18:13:52.736Z · final_answer

Yes. My local wait is stopped now; Apple is still processing the submission server-side.

In Terminal, check it with:

```bash
xcrun notarytool info 562f137b-96d8-4e94-a06c-989c11caf649 --keychain-profile sage-notary
```

When it says `status: Accepted`, run:

```bash
APP="/Users/naomiivie/bethel/email-agent/desktop/src-tauri/target/release/bundle/macos/Sage.app"

xcrun stapler staple "$APP"
xcrun stapler validate "$APP"
spctl --assess --type execute --verbose=4 "$APP"
```

Then make the DMG:

```bash
RELEASE="/Users/naomiivie/bethel/email-agent/release"
DMG="$RELEASE/Sage_1.0.0_aarch64.dmg"
IDENTITY="Developer ID Application: Naomi Ivie (7RTTC5R7ZQ)"

rm -f "$DMG"
hdiutil create -volname "Sage" -srcfolder "$APP" -ov -format UDZO "$DMG"
codesign --force --timestamp --sign "$IDENTITY" "$DMG"
```

Then notarize the DMG too:

```bash
xcrun notarytool submit "$DMG" --keychain-profile sage-notary --wait
xcrun stapler staple "$DMG"
xcrun stapler validate "$DMG"
spctl --assess --type open --context context:primary-signature --verbose=4 "$DMG"
```

If the app submission says `Invalid`, fetch the reason with:

```bash
xcrun notarytool log 562f137b-96d8-4e94-a06c-989c11caf649 --keychain-profile sage-notary
```

Right now it still says `In Progress`, so the next move is just to rerun the first `info` command until Apple returns `Accepted`.

## Naomi · 2026-06-22T18:20:12.877Z

should I createa new one? how on gdoes this typically take is it a human checking?

## Codex · 2026-06-22T18:20:40.931Z · commentary

No, I would **not create a new submission yet**. A second upload usually does not make this faster; it just creates another notarization job for Apple to process, and then we have to track which artifact is the real release. The right move is to wait on `562f137b-96d8-4e94-a06c-989c11caf649` until it says `Accepted` or `Invalid`.

It is not a human App Store review. Apple describes notarization as an automated security/code-signing scan for Developer ID software distributed outside the Mac App Store. Apple’s current doc says the scan “usually takes less than an hour,” and their WWDC notarytool talk says the service runs automated analysis for malware/signing issues. Sources: [Apple notarizing macOS software](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/security/notarizing-macos-software-before-distribution), [Apple WWDC notarytool overview](https://developer.apple.com/videos/play/wwdc2021/10261/).

Since ours was submitted at `2026-06-22T17:47:55Z`, I’d give it the full hour before worrying. If it’s still stuck well past that, we check Apple system status/history, then decide whether to cancel our wait and resubmit a fresh zip.

## Codex · 2026-06-22T21:07:57.528Z · final_answer

No, I would **not create a new one** right now.

If Apple already accepted the upload and gave you submission `562f137b-96d8-4e94-a06c-989c11caf649`, that means the signing/notarization request made it into Apple’s system. Creating another Developer ID cert or another submission usually just adds confusion unless this one fails.

For macOS notarization, “processing” is typically **automated**, not a human App Review. Apple checks the signature, hardened runtime, entitlements, malware scan, bundle structure, etc. Normal timing is usually **a few minutes**, sometimes **10-30 minutes**, occasionally longer if Apple’s queue is slow.

I’d wait about **30-60 minutes** before assuming anything is wrong. If it fails, we read the notary log and fix the exact issue. If it succeeds, we staple the ticket to the app/DMG and you’re good.

The only separate thing you may still need to do manually is accept the latest **Program License Agreement** in the Apple Developer account if Xcode shows that PLA warning. That is account-side access, not a reason to create a new certificate.

## Codex · 2026-06-22T21:17:17.178Z · final_answer

Done. Sage now has deployed Stripe payment webhook operations.

What changed:
- Added `POST /api/stripe-webhook` using Stripe’s official Node SDK signature verification.
- Paid `checkout.session.completed` events create private GitHub onboarding issues labeled `phase-0` + `payment`.
- Failed invoices / canceled or past-due subscriptions create `billing` + `support` issues.
- Added `npm run payment:triage` and wired it into the Monday owner-review automation.
- Added `ops/runbooks/stripe-webhook.md`, updated billing/deployment/readiness docs, and updated issues #2, #4, and #6.
- Created GitHub labels `payment` and `billing`.
- Deployed production commit `fb0468b`.

Verified:
- `npm test` passes: 7/7.
- `npm run typecheck` passes.
- Signed Stripe webhook smoke test passed locally with mocked GitHub.
- Live `/api/status` is on `fb0468b`.
- Live `/api/stripe-webhook` safely returns `405` for `GET` and `503 stripe_webhook_not_configured` until secrets exist.
- Phase 0 readiness is now `10/15`.

Still waiting on account-side setup:
- `GITHUB_ISSUE_TOKEN` in Vercel, issue #4.
- `FOUNDING_CHECKOUT_URL` + `STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET`, issue #6.
- Sentry DSN/probe verification, issue #1.
- Google OAuth/CASA owned-domain gates, issue #5.

Best next move: do issue #4 first, because that one token unlocks lead capture, support capture, and payment/billing issue capture.

Stripe docs checked: [webhooks](https://docs.stripe.com/webhooks), [webhook quickstart](https://docs.stripe.com/webhooks/quickstart), [Payment Link URL parameters](https://docs.stripe.com/payment-links/url-parameters), [after a Payment Link payment](https://docs.stripe.com/payment-links/post-payment).

## Codex · 2026-06-22T21:41:36.625Z · final_answer

No, don’t create a new one right now.

If Apple accepted the upload and gave us submission `562f137b-96d8-4e94-a06c-989c11caf649`, that means the cert/API access got far enough. Creating another Developer ID cert or API key won’t make processing faster, and it can make the signing setup messier.

For macOS Developer ID notarization, it is usually automated: Apple scans the signed app for signing validity, hardened runtime/notarization requirements, and known malware. It is not normally a human App Store-style review.

Typical timing:

- Common: a few minutes to 15 minutes
- Still normal: 30-60 minutes
- Annoying but possible: a few hours, especially if Apple’s queue is backed up
- If it sits overnight, then we should treat it as stuck or failed-to-report and check logs/support

What I’d do now: wait a bit, then check status. If it fails, we fetch the notarization log, fix the exact issue, and upload a new build. If it’s still “Processing,” we leave it alone.

## Codex · 2026-06-22T22:55:11.519Z · final_answer

No, don’t create a new one yet. If Apple accepted the upload and says it’s processing, we should wait on that same upload/build/submission. Creating a new app/build right now can make the App Store Connect state messier.

Typical timing:

- Build/upload processing: usually minutes to maybe an hour. Apple says if a build stays in `Processing` for more than 24 hours, that may indicate an issue and you should contact Apple or file feedback.
- App Review after submission: Apple says 90% of submissions are reviewed in under 24 hours.
- Human checking: the `processing` part is mostly automated validation/scanning/ingest. The actual App Review step is Apple review, with humans involved and automated assistance.

So: wait for now. If it is still processing after a few hours, I’d check App Store Connect for errors. If it hits 24 hours, then we re-upload or contact Apple depending on the exact status.

## Codex · 2026-06-22T23:22:14.990Z · commentary

Don’t create a new submission yet. That UUID looks like Apple notarization, not App Store human review: notarization is mostly automated scanning/signature validation, and Apple’s docs say most notarizations finish within minutes, while another Apple page frames it as usually under an hour. App Store Review is the human/hybrid step and Apple says 90% are reviewed in under 24 hours, but that is a different process.

For this one: wait 30-60 minutes unless we know the uploaded app/package is wrong. If it is still “In Progress” after an hour or two, I’d check status again before resubmitting; if it goes many hours, it may be Apple queue latency, not something you caused.
